ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U4	Honourable Riigikogu! Let's keep going. The question is this: 'What approximate number of local governments will remain in Estonia after the administrative reform promised by the Triple Association? Is it 50, 100, 150 or more? ' I hope that my previous answers already gave more or less an idea of what this reform would be like. The number of municipalities is determined by which criteria are set, how high we raise the bar. But I believe that the name of the reform deserves this story if we still have less than 100 municipalities. The second question: 'When will the government coalition restore the municipal income base and local road funding to pre-crisis levels, and in what form and to what extent will the revenue that has not been received by the municipalities in the intervening years be compensated? ' The total revenue of the 2014 local government budget exceeded and significantly exceeded the peak year of the economic boom, the 2008 level. Between 2015 and 2020 local authorities will receive additional EU Structural Funds grants of around EUR 900 million. The coalition agreement aims to increase road maintenance support and introduce a measure to support recreational activities. Strengthening local government budgets does not necessarily have to be done by increasing the share of income tax or tea money; there are other possibilities. As the financial opportunities of less well-off municipalities are lagging behind those of the non-recipients of the Equalisation Fund, it is more reasonable to increase the income base through the Equalisation Fund. The government and coalition partners have discussed that if we have the opportunity to give more money to local governments, it would rather be through the Equalisation Fund. This objective has been highlighted in the additions to the Coalition Agreement and these options will be reviewed in the preparation of the state budget strategy. During the economic boom, the State had the opportunity to increase the income tax share of the municipalities, the equalisation fund and the road maintenance allowance. I'm telling you, it was during the economic boom. In 2009, cuts had to be made in all sectors to ensure the country's financial sustainability. The European Government Charter says that local government funds must be in line with national economic policies. It is certainly possible to return to the percentage of income tax when the country's finances improve. What I want to say right now is that I understand why the opposition keeps reminding me of this percentage of income tax. However, if, for example, we look at the same 11,6 that is currently in force, this was also in force in 2005, ten years ago, but at that time a financial amount of EUR 406 million was received, and now the exact same percentage point of EUR 878 million is received. I believe that, in fact, the mayors are still interested in money and the money comes more than twice as much as the same percentage. And also from the booming level where the percentage was 11.9, the current level is already EUR 144 million in financial terms. It's not going so bad in that sense. The third question: 'What additional measures is the government coalition planning to implement to increase the financial autonomy of local authorities? ' Some simple things. Firstly, in the course of the administrative reform, the formula for the distribution of the equalisation fund will be reviewed with a view to reducing its demotivating impact on job creation and tax revenue growth. Secondly, it is considered necessary to increase the total amount of the Equalisation Fund. Thirdly, to analyse the possibilities of increasing the financial autonomy of local authorities by extending the right to impose local taxes. It also analyses how to reduce the ad hoc nature of subsidies granted from the state budget to municipalities and, where possible, to increase support resources to the local government's income tax share and equalisation fund. Consideration will be given to linking the revenue of local governments with the jobs in the territory to motivate them to engage more in the business environment. The share of income tax that has been reallocated would correspond to the additional burden associated with the development of the business environment. Detailed amendments are also discussed by members of the government, the panel of experts, as well as by different administrative areas. ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U30	Thank you. I ask the Riigikogu's speech chair to answer the question of the Minister of State Administration Arto Asa!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U31	Honourable guests! I'm a little embarrassed because I have a very long and technical answer. Now, do you read it or make it more humanized according to the time of day? But let's get started. The first question: 'Is the bureaucratic excesses in public procurement due to the regulations introduced by the European Union and whether the countries belonging to the European Union have any levers to influence this legislation and to introduce derogations affecting specific areas of life. If the Republic of Estonia has such opportunities, have they actually been used or tried to use them? ' I am saying very simply that it is quite difficult to find a balance in public procurement and to balance different interests. On the one hand, public procurement may appear to be a formal and cumbersome procedure, but it has a single reason in the public interest: to ensure that public funds are used in a uniform manner, so that the procedure is public, transparent and proportionate, not to mention the sustainable and rational use of money. The functioning of the European Union and the decision-making process should probably be felt by those in this Chamber. The Riigikogu and the European Union Affairs Committee always have the opportunity to have a say in establishing directives. I can assure you from experience that in our positions we have always emphasised that there could be less bureaucracy. We see that rules are often developed on the basis of larger countries and different systems. We are small, we have simple business and we would like the rules of the European Union to be that way. But the functioning of the European Union is one of the great art of finding compromises, and our interests do not always coincide with those of others. However, the Riigikogu will soon have the opportunity to discuss the law on public procurement. We will shortly bring a new version of the Public Procurement Act to the Riigikogu. This need arises from the new public procurement directives of the European Union. Even during the negotiation of directives, all parties were involved, but the law is, of course, more specific. It must be said, however, that the area of public procurement is quite precisely, at times very precisely regulated in European Union law. From the point of view of the European Union, it is essential to ensure an internal market without borders is one of the most important principles of the European Union and to balance the narrow and sometimes protectionist interests of the Member States. Therefore, also in those places where the European Union leaves discretion in its directives, it is often written, between which choices or within which the State can exercise that discretion and make distinctions. However, more specifically, universities can make use of a number of different features of public procurement, both under current law and under the forthcoming draft. For example, to organise negotiated procedures without prior publication of a contract notice for the procurement of items produced solely for the purposes of research, experimentation, study or development, or to order a variety of educational and study-related services under a simplified procedure. It also follows from the Directive and the law that universities are not required to organise public procurement when ordering research and development services, provided that the study does not benefit only the university and the services ordered are not fully remunerated by the university. In addition, the Directives give Member States the right to find a more self-appropriate solution in certain aspects, but, as has been said, such options come from the Directive itself. Thus, the levers of public procurement law available to the Member States are often limited to what the European Union legislator has explicitly envisaged as a choice. The second question: 'How do you assess the proposals put forward by universities to introduce new additional regulations that would help reduce bureaucratic confusion about public procurement? ' More specifically, a binding reference for a preliminary ruling is requested, as well as the possibility of approving a sectoral purchasing guide in the Ministry of Responsibility. While the honourable questioner mentioned that universities had to employ lawyers to chew through all this regulation, last week's meeting with representatives of universities made such statements that they had more than 200 people at work who had to deal with procurement. Indeed, this mass was also surprising to me, and it is certainly very seriously argued problems. What can we say? We can say with certainty that we have been in constant contact with universities, even before this specific draft was dealt with and coordinated, there have been meetings with representatives of universities. I met them again last week, i.e. for the first time in this job. In particular, as regards the binding reference for a preliminary ruling that was asked about, our analysis says that, given Estonia's and the European judicial area, it does not do too much good. As has emerged from the discussion with universities, the need for a preliminary ruling arises in particular from the fact that, from time to time, situations arise in which different authorities interpret the law on public procurement inconsistently. This is particularly the case in the context of the sharing of structural resources. The Ministry of Finance understands this concern, but above all, we see the problem as binding. If, for example, the preliminary ruling were binding only on the Ministry of Finance but not on the implementing entity, it would clearly not help to mitigate the risk of possible recovery. It is important to understand that the Structural Grants Act and the Public Procurement Act are different in nature and are based on different legal frameworks established by the European Union, which is why the Ministry of Finance cannot, when interpreting the Public Procurement Act, give binding decisions to institutions controlling the use of structural resources. It is likely that the introduction of the possibility to apply for a preliminary ruling would not have a significant impact on the volume of challenge to public procurement, since an exequatur binding by the executive would not be able to deprive a person of the right to effective redress in the public procurement appeal committee or court. The current view is that the preliminary ruling procedure would not help universities much closer to their objective. At the same time, we are ready to offer all the help we can in minimising the interpretations of the Public Procurement Act. It is planned to continue looking for solutions in the interaction between structural support institutions and universities. The Ministry of Finance will also continue to provide free advice on public procurement within the framework of the new law, as well as providing for the possibility that contracting authorities can request the Ministry of Finance to coordinate their procurement procedures, which should facilitate the conduct of public procurement and increase legal certainty. So we offer some solutions within this new law. The third question: 'What are the prospects for normalising public procurement procedures for structural grants so that the specificities of the services to be procured can be taken into account? " In short, that was the question. In fact, the answer lies in the core of the whole problem: we have two different regulations coming from the European Union. One is about public procurement, and we are changing that procedure, raising the thresholds and trying to cut red tape. The other side is structural instruments; the European Union's money here is another regulation, tougher rules. These are different procedures and are based on different legal frameworks. There is no special procedure for public procurement within the meaning of the Structural Grants Act, and following the transposition of directives, the Public Procurement Act is the first act. The few differences concern only contracting entities, which, as far as I am aware, are of benefit in the case of universities. Moreover, in the absence of regulation under the Public Procurement Act, it is possible to impose obligations on contracting authorities, which are a separate talk, in the terms of granting the aid. But what makes universities the most of their problems is that the Structural Support Act sets a threshold of EUR 5 000. Indeed, EUR 5 000 is very little. In the Public Procurement Act, we will go to higher thresholds in the future. This ceiling of EUR 5000 is set on the basis of the principles of the financial statute applicable to the general budget of the European Union. To sum up briefly, with this work, which is my area of administration and which concerns the procedure and the law for public procurement, we may not have a very big problem. Structural grants, which formally also belong to the field of Minister of Finance Sven Sester and are dealt with by other departments of the Ministry of Finance, are more complex. But I believe that some solutions are possible here, so these regulations should not get in the way of each other. What is the point in one law to set limits if, in the other, they remain exactly the same? So work with universities goes on. We had a meeting where, yes, we really discussed public procurement in particular. As the main problem seems to be in structural support and regulations, we have to do the next meeting, hopefully, now that we have people at the table dealing with structural support problems. Let's see if there's anything going on. I tried to simplify this approach and speak in human language. Thank you.

' In the coalition agreement, you have promised to lay down the duties of local governments at the level of the Act and regulate the activities of joint local governments so that local governments can ensure the provision of services evenly throughout Estonia. ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U38	I ask the Riigikogu Chair to answer the question of the Minister of State Administration Arto Asa!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U39	Honourable Riigikogu! The answers to this question are much shorter, and I will read them quickly. The first question: 'How do you intend to comply with the decision of the General Assembly of the Supreme Court of 16 March 2010 on the definition and financing of the constitutional duties and other tasks of local governments, including those imposed by state authority? When will the duties of local governments be laid down and specified at law level? " The public tasks carried out by the municipalities are exceptional and the municipality is in the role of the State's so-called extension of arm's length, in compliance with the prescribed guidelines with very little discretion. For such tasks, the state uses the help of local governments because their employees deal directly with people's concerns and have better information about local residents. The money involved in these tasks is 2% of the cost of the municipality's core business. § 51 of the State Budget Act obliges the annual state budget to distinguish the money intended for the performance of public tasks from the money used for local government tasks. The explanatory memorandum to the state budget shall provide information on the amount of resources to be provided to cover the costs of each public task and on the giver. Thus, the funding system provided by the Supreme Court has been established by law. In order to distinguish national tasks from self-governing, the law adds to the task "State task carried out by the own government'. If there is no such indication, it is a self-governing task which is not further marked. National tasks have been defined as the registration of births and deaths, the payment of subsistence allowances and need-based family allowances, and the transport of unknown dead persons to expertise. The review of tasks and the definition of the task as state or self-governance is ongoing. Of course, it should be added that there is probably no universal methodology or formula for determining the adequacy of the income base anywhere in the world. There's always less money than wishes. It can be confirmed that the Ministry of Finance is also analysing the cost side of local tasks during the current reforms. For each task, the Ministry of Finance requires the calculation of the cost and the indication of the sources of cover. The second question: 'Will you and when are you planning to restore the funding base for municipalities (income tax, equalisation fund, road-saving funds, etc.), which was significantly reduced in 2009? " This question has already been answered here. It is not going to be repeated that these funds can come from elsewhere. This coalition considers regional policy more appropriate to increase the fine-tuning fund when this possibility arises. In addition, EUR 900 million of European Union funds have been mentioned. So I believe that whoever listened to the debate earlier received this answer. I can also explain why the coalition has made such a choice that, when money is generated, it is rather preferable to changing the tax rate to the fine-tuning fund. A quick analysis says that if you change the tax rate, more than half of this money goes to Harjumaa. With its golden circle, Tallinn and Harjumaa would receive more than half of this extra money, which is probably not the smartest choice in regional policy. The money from the Equalisation Fund still goes to those who are on the outskirts and who are struggling to cope. The granting of additional funding to Viimsi and Rae rural municipality is unlikely to be critical in terms of limited resources. The third question: 'In the local government's income base, independent income accounts for 1%. Do you think that such a situation is in line with the principles of the European Charter for Local Government regarding the financial autonomy of municipalities? How can independent organisation and decision-making be carried out without an independent income base on issues relating to the local environment, services and well-being, as well as the tasks imposed by the state by law? 'The share of local taxes in the principal income of local governments is indeed 1%. At the same time, according to the methodology widely used for assessing financial autonomy, the land tax is essentially a local tax. The fact that local governments have no right to set the rate of income tax does not in any way limit the autonomy of local governments when using income tax receipts. The municipality's income is targeted at 23% and the remaining 77% of the resources are decided independently by the municipality without State interference. The government coalition is committed to reducing the proportion of targeted support in the future. The European Charter of Local Government does not determine the scope of the financial autonomy of the municipalities; it is a national issue. There are countries where the share of targeted support is higher and there are countries where it is smaller. It is larger in countries such as the Czech Republic, Slovenia and Hungary, and smaller in Finland, Sweden and Norway. As has been said earlier, I personally advocate that if we make our choices and change financing schemes and administrative arrangements, we could move towards increasing the proportion of local authorities. Thank you.

The inquiry was submitted on 10 June 2015 concerning the maintenance of institutions financed by the state budget for membership of the European Union and bearing the number 53. ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U53	Dear Deputy Speaker of the Riigikogu, Honourable Members! Dear representative of the Social Democratic Party of the coalition, it is a pleasure! Great Britain has calculated the cost of maintaining the bureaucratic machine of the institutions and authorities coming from the European Union. This is, according to the various figures, 5 to 7% of GDP, corresponding to the year and the accounting methodology. We expect every country in the European Union to be aware of how much the state budget and GDP actually takes to keep this bureaucratic machine running. Has an internal audit been carried out to find out how much it takes to maintain the institutions and posts arising from the European Union from the budget of the Estonian state? ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U54	Thank you. I ask the Riigikogu's speech chair, Minister of State Administration Arto Asa, to answer the question, which bears number 53.
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U55	Honourable Riigikogu! Good heirs! Since you've been so bravely waiting for my answer, I'll report it in full. The first question concerned whether there has been an audit of how much the budget of the Estonian state spends on maintaining the institutions and posts of the European Union. The answer is that no audit has been carried out by the Ministry of Finance or the National Audit Office to give an overview of the costs of all functions related to the European Union. A mere cost-benefit analysis would not ensure a comprehensive view of the benefits of belonging to the European Union or of the efficiency of spending money. It is also difficult to assess which posts are only costing because of membership of the European Union, because we do not have a comparison of the positions that the country should hold if Estonia were not a member of the European Union, but should cooperate with the European Union and its Member States. Since 2005, the effectiveness of the implementation system for structural funds has been assessed, and this cost has been assessed twice by audit and consultancy firms. In 2011, the mid-term evaluation of the structural instruments indicated, inter alia, that the implementation system had spent 2,03 cents on the disbursement of one of the support kroons. The kroons were still in force at the time, but the order of magnitude is reached: it took 2.03 cents for a hundred units. A large part of the resources of the implementation system will be spent on the control of payment applications, the processing of aid applications and the development and modification of support policies and measures. Compared to our closest neighbours Finland, Latvia and Lithuania, the implementation of structural funds in Estonia can be considered effective. Both the volume of support administered and the volume of payments per full-time employee are the largest in Estonia. However, it is always possible to increase efficiency by systematically collecting information on where the resources of the implementation system are spent. As the annual analysis would be too resource-intensive, the studies will be carried out over a few years. In 2010, the Structural Funds administration had a total of 395,6 full-time employees and the implementation of these funds cost 170 million kroons, or 10.86 million euros. The 2010 study did not include costs for PRIA, they are presented separately. The figures for 2015 used in the PRIA are 370 employees and cost EUR 11.5 million. Since Estonia's accession to the European Union, an average of 770 people have worked in the system of application of grants per year and they have spent an average of 20 million euros. This represents a total of €247.8 million for the whole 2004-2015 period. The Ministry of Finance has explained the costs of entities related to the European Union in the areas of government of the Ministries and the costs of staff working in foreign representations of the European Union and their spouses. In addition to the implementation of the structural instruments, there are still some 280 staff members of public institutions who are related to the European Union. It can also be pointed out that 227 staff are employed in the European Union's foreign missions and 124 workers are employed abroad. A total of approximately 415 million euros has been spent in Estonia to support almost 1400 jobs. This expenditure represents 0.2% of GDP. The second question: 'Is this expenditure justified and do you see the possibility of reducing expenditure in this area? ' Compared to the 7% shown in the United Kingdom, Estonia's cost is not high, given the benefits that membership has brought us. We can talk here about the overall increase in the credibility of the economy, the country's credit rating, the free movement of goods and services, the development of the economy, the right of expression in the conduct of monetary policy, the loss of currency risk, lower interest rates, price stability, lower transaction costs, better price comparison and, of course, the positive effects of the use of European funds on competitiveness and balanced development. The third question: 'If you see opportunities to reduce these costs, then at what expense and to what extent? 'The austerity measures for the implementation of future budgetary periods or structural funds for the current period are as follows: The European Commission has taken into account in its assessment of the impact of the cohesion policy drafts for the period 2014 to 2020 that, if a Member State makes use of all the simplifications foreseen for the period under EU rules and to the maximum, the average savings for beneficiaries could be 20% on average and 7% on average for Member State administrations compared to the previous period. This was the European Commission's assessment of how much savings the European Union can make on average. Since Estonia already has optimal systems for applying subsidies, we do not have so much savings to expect, but the system can, of course, be improved and we intend to use all the possibilities to do this more effectively. For example, things that have already been implemented. In the new period 2014-2020, Estonia has one operational programme instead of three. Consequently, one monitoring report must be drawn up instead of the three so far and one assurance of financial management, which will reduce bureaucracy and duplication between ministries. Its financial impact has not yet been calculated, but it is certainly significant. From the beginning of the following year, there must be opportunities for electronic communication between grant applicants and beneficiaries and the administration authorities. Under the possibilities created, a gradual transition to the use of electronic application environments will take place. In the long term, the electronic environment will save staff costs and reduce the burden on beneficiaries both when applying for support and when implementing the process. Of course, in the case of electronic solutions, the costs of ICT should be taken into account. The European Commission has considered that, on average, the introduction of IT solutions in the European Union could save up to 11% of the administrative burden. In Estonia, this percentage is probably lower, as we have already taken certain steps in the field of technology. In addition, the new European rules allow simplified cost reimbursement methods for projects below EUR 50000. The introduction of simplified methods will help to reduce the administrative burden both on the beneficiaries who do not have to provide cost documents and on the administration side, as expenditure documents do not need to be checked. The substantive principle of simplified reimbursement methods is that the grant is not paid on the basis of statements of expenditure but on the basis of the results achieved. The simplified system also allows for the payment of overheads, either as a proportion of staff costs or as a proportion of the total cost of the project, which will significantly reduce the administrative burden. In the new period, the CPR states that projects below a certain threshold (EUR 200000 for the Cohesion Fund, EUR 150000 for the European Social Fund, EUR 100000 for the EMFF) will not be audited at national level more than once during their lifetime. There is a plan to simplify the implementation system by reducing the number of agencies and centralising tasks. For example, in the field of transport, all the functions of the implementing unit were grouped together in the Technical Surveillance Authority and the Road Administration and the Maritime Administration no longer perform these functions of the implementing unit. Now this competence is in one place, which is definitely a place of austerity. Another example: Last September, the implementation unit of the Ministry of Social Affairs was brought to the Ministry of Finance, and again it is no longer necessary to operate in several places. This year, the implementing entities will also be transferred to the Ministry of Finance in the context of the financial instrument measures previously managed by KredEx. So we are looking for ways to cut red tape. Certain rules have been agreed in the European Union as a whole and cannot pass them, but one comma of you can certainly be done better. Even in this picture, Estonia has been very effective, not only on the expenditure side, in the sense of how much it takes us to get one euro, but we are one of the best at all who has used up the funds. If we look at a very big picture, then in the previous period all our costs were EUR 2.3 billion and the European Union became EUR 6.4 billion. Even if we exclude all indirect positive effects, the direct financial report is clearly positive. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U90	Dear Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, Dear Governor of Eesti Pank, On behalf of ten members of the Riigikogu, at the beginning of the summer we briefed the Governor of Eesti Pank on the asset purchase programme of the central banks of the euro area.
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U91	One moment, please, Kadri Simson! Please stop the clock! Honourable members of the Riigikogu, please be quiet! Please, it's okay to go on!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U92	I understand that 1.1 trillion euros are very exciting in the members of the Riigikogu, because there are so many zeros in this number that it is unfathomable. But from s. From 9 March 2004 the central banks started buying-in bonds in the European Union. Among other things, our own Estonian central bank will also contribute to this buying-in. The aim of buying-in bonds is primarily to contribute to the economic growth of countries whose bonds are purchased but also to help restore sustainable inflation. Government bonds are also purchased from the private sector, i.e. from the secondary market. We here in Estonia are in an exceptional situation. Our country has no bonds and therefore Eesti Pank buys most of the bonds of European international organisations, but since June it has also been entitled to purchase the bonds of the state-owned network company Elering AS. These EUR 1.1 trillion are divided into a year and a half. The bond purchases started in March will last until September next year. On the basis of the capital key, Eesti Pank's monthly purchase is roughly 120 million euros. We also looked at the website of Eesti Pank today. There are still only the first three months of purchases: 117 million euros in March, 119 million euros in April and 129.8 million euros in May. A total of 365 million euros has been purchased in three months. The Governor of Eesti Pank will certainly specify the purchases of the last few months. That is why we have a number of questions. We asked these questions in the spring to the Prime Minister, who either did not want or could not provide answers. He pointed out that what kind of bonds are purchased is the question of Eesti Pank's own choices. We therefore want to know what these purchases are made on the basis of and under what conditions this activity must meet. We want an assessment of whether Estonia would be in a better position if, like other countries in the euro area, we could also revive our own economies. Thank you for your attention and I look forward to the answers.
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U93	Ardo Hansson, Governor of Eesti Pank, must answer the question. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U94	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Honourable Riigikogu! I have been asked three very constructive questions that I am trying to answer today. First, it asked which countries and international organisations had the bonds purchased by Eesti Pank under the asset purchase programme of the central banks of the euro area. The second question was which conditions the bond must satisfy in order for Eesti Pank to invest in it. The Eurosystem central banks' asset purchase programme shall comprise three purchase programmes. First, the financial sector covered bonds, in English covered bonds. Secondly, asset-backed bonds, in English asset-backed securities. These two are private-sector bonds. Thirdly, public sector bonds or public sector purchase programmes. What is the purpose of the asset purchase programme, and why do we do so? The central bank buys up bonds on the market to encourage investors selling bonds to invest money elsewhere. Purchases have the effect of reducing the return on lower-risk assets and this motivates investors to take higher risks. This so-called portfolio relocation effect contributes to the growth of investment and thus economic activity. As the economy recovers, inflation will also gradually come closer to the common objective of the euro area central banks, which is to keep inflation in the euro area on average just below 2% in the medium term. The central banks have reached asset purchases because interest rates traditionally used to manage economic activity and inflation have reached zero and are no longer able to influence the economy. Since the issues focus on the public bond purchase programme of central banks, and this has also raised the most questions in the public sphere, I will explain this in more detail. First of all, I would like to stress that the financial system in continental Europe is bank-based and that a fully unified capital market is still at an early stage of development. This means that European companies mainly finance themselves with bank loans, and that is why the bond market here is much smaller than the United States, where most of the financing of business is provided through the bond market. The European bond market is therefore rather limited. More than half of this market is made up of euro area governments and institutional bonds when it comes to debt securities suitable for central banks. A sufficiently large market was one of the important reasons why the Governing Council of the European Central Bank decided to intervene in the market for public sector bonds with regard to asset purchases. In order not to unduly affect this market by central banks, we have made it a restriction that euro area central banks can buy up to a third of the volume of issuer bonds. Each week, the European Central Bank publishes a summary of the expanded asset purchase programmes of the euro area central banks on its website. Purchases of the euro area central banks' public sector bond purchase programme are made every month for about 50 billion euros, of which Eesti Pank accounts for around 120 million euros. The total value of bonds purchased under the public securities purchase programme amounted to EUR 314,5 billion as at 11 September. Planned asset purchases until September 2016 or until the Governing Council sees inflation dynamics consistent with the mandate of the euro area central banks to keep inflation in the euro area below and close to 2% over the medium term. As a general rule, each central bank buys its own government bonds as part of a public bond purchase programme, while other sovereign bonds are not purchased. Purchases require these bonds to be accepted as collateral in monetary policy operations of the euro area central banks, which means having a sufficiently strong credit rating. The credit risk associated with the purchase of own government bonds is borne by the national central bank. As the Estonian state has not issued bonds, Eesti Pank will make so-called replacement purchases within the limits of the volume allocated to us. The central banks also make replacement purchases in countries where the offer of bonds corresponding to the purchase plan is limited. Eesti Pank generally buys bonds from European institutions, such as the European Financial Stability Fund, the Nordic Investment Bank and the European Investment Bank. The list of eligible bonds for buy-in purchases shall be approved by the Governing Council of the European Central Bank. Non-financial corporations belonging to the public sector can be transferred there in exceptional circumstances. Eesti Pank asked the Governing Council of the European Central Bank for permission to exceptionally buy Elering's bonds belonging to the Estonian state and this was granted at the beginning of June. The purchase of Elering's bonds is treated by Eesti Pank as an option, but not as an obligation. Specific purchasing volumes depend on the availability and price of the asset. As of the end of August, Eesti Pank had purchased Elering's bonds in the order of 30 million euros. It should be stressed that the volume of bonds issued by Elering is quite small in view of Eesti Pank's purchasing volumes and the offer of these bonds on the market is even more limited. If we were to buy (and sell) Elering's bonds at the maximum amount allowed to take into account the issue limit of the asset purchase programme, this would cover just over half of the volume of one month's purchases of Eesti Pank's asset purchase programme. I now come to the third question, which reads as follows: 'The European Central Bank's asset purchase programme is intended to boost the European Union's economy. The Estonian state does not have state bonds, so Eesti Pank does not have the possibility to buy its own government bonds. How does this fact affect the impact of the plan on the Estonian economy? Is this why the impact of the programme on the Estonian economy less than in countries that buy their own government bonds under the programme? ' First of all, I stress that the public bond purchase programme follows a government financing ban. Therefore, bonds will never be purchased on the primary market, i.e. directly from the government, but only from the secondary market after the post-issue market price has developed. In this way, the price of government credit risk will be left to other market participants to shape. Secondly, it is important to note that Eesti Pank buys Elering's bonds belonging to the Estonian state. At the same time, Elering is not directly benefiting from it, because as a central bank, we buy bonds only on the secondary market. Thus, Eesti Pank can buy Elering's bonds from a bank located in London, for example, or from a client of a bank operating in Estonia. By buying Elering's bonds, we'll make sure we get the best deal we can on the market. The same principle applies to government bonds. For example, the German national central bank does not buy its sovereign bonds from the government but from the secondary market. So if a bank operating in Estonia or an investor in Estonia holds bonds issued by a government of a euro area country, banks or investors operating in Estonia can also sell them to the central bank of that country. Thirdly, I would stress that the Governing Council of the European Central Bank decided to purchase assets in order to encourage investors selling bonds to invest money elsewhere, which means better financing conditions for companies operating in the euro area, including Estonian companies. The asset purchase programme cannot be the reason why one country increases the debt burden on its government. Under the asset purchase programme, the bond holder will change but the volume of bonds issued by the countries will not grow. Overall, a reduction in public debt in the euro area as a whole is essential. In the euro area, the government debt ratio is projected to fall from 92% of GDP last year to 89% of GDP by 2017. One of the strengths of Estonia has been the low level of public debt. When planning financial matters, we must take into account the quite sudden ageing of the Estonian population, which is why we have fewer hands and more people needing support. In addition, as the level of income in the country increases, EU subsidies will start to decrease. On the eve of more financial times, it cannot be considered reasonable to increase debt. In making plans for financing the state budget, it is important to be guided by both European Union rules and national laws that provide for structural budgetary balances. A rapidly increasing public debt without a significant reason would probably lead to a downgrading of Estonia's sovereign rating and would significantly worsen the financial conditions of companies and private individuals. The asset purchase programme cannot be the main reason for public investment either. Public investment must earn money borrowed in accordance with both commercial logic and European Union rules, because the debt must be repaid regardless of who the creditor is. Given the current cyclical position of the Estonian economy, there is no need to issue additional bonds to finance the Estonian state budget. According to Eesti Pank, the Estonian economy is currently at a stage where the government should start saving rather than borrowing more for additional expenditure. As a result of the purchase of assets, lending will become more favourable to Estonian companies, households and also to the government. Thus, Estonia will also benefit from short-term asset purchases, although we do not have government bonds that the central bank could buy. Further account should be taken of the impact of monetary policy rates in the euro area and other countries on exchange rates. Looking at the forecasts of the major developed countries, it is clear that in the coming years the euro area is expected to continue to support growth, but in several other developing countries interest rates are projected to rise. This difference in growth prospects has led to a weakening of the exchange rate of the euro. The cheap euro supports the export opportunities of euro area countries. Improved competitiveness will benefit Estonia at least in the short term, where exports account for more than 75% of total production and nearly half of exports relate to non-euro area countries. Interest has never been as low in Europe or around the world as it is now. A favourable interest environment supports the restoration of economic activity. However, persistently low interest rates can also lead to imbalances, i.e. risks which can be costly for society to realise. Possible bubbles in financial markets or real estate prices are more likely to occur with low interest rates. This is why it is more important than ever for macro-financial supervision and for efforts to improve and develop macro-financial supervision, both national and European. That is why Eesti Pank has also increased the buffers required of banks and set minimum requirements for commercial banks to grant housing loans, which will reduce the risk of a loan boom in the future as a preventive step. Thus, all in all, Estonian companies and households will be able to benefit from the asset purchase programme, but this will not lead to temptations to raise public debt in a situation where we should actually reduce it in view of the cyclical position of the economy. It must be borne in mind that monetary policy can provide short-term relief for the economy, but it cannot generate long-term economic growth. Thus, monetary policy measures do not replace the need for structural reforms to improve competitiveness and to maintain public finances in order. Thank you for your attention. I'm ready to answer your questions.

ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U110	The inquiry is answered by Prime Minister Taavi Kõivas. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U111	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! I will respond to the question of 11 May on the taxation of banks. Question one: '2013. The profits of Estonian commercial banks amounted to 443.6 million euros in 2008. Even if all profits had been taxed at a rate of 12% immediately after the profits were made, more than EUR 53 million would have been paid to the state budget instead of EUR 17,4 million. Why are banks clearly undertaxed in Estonia? ' Since all questions of inquiry concern the Estonian income tax system, it is worth pointing out from the outset that income tax, especially corporate income tax, is the most disincentive to economic activity, according to empirical analyses. Consumption taxes hinder economic activity less than income tax, asset taxes even less. This observation comes not from me, but from the OECD. It is also stressed that a growth-friendly tax system distorts as little as possible market signals and that undistorted market signals contribute to a more efficient allocation of resources in the economy. Coming to the question asked, it should be noted that commercial banks operating in Estonia are not undertaxed. Estonia has a tax system under which profits of companies are taxed only when they are paid out. For example, this summer Swedbank made a dividend payment of EUR 400 million, from which EUR 100 million of income tax was collected in the state budget. The Income Tax Act has encouraged Estonian banks to hold buffers and thus made them stronger. Secondly, unlike companies in the non-financial sector, banks have to pay not only normal taxes but also fees aimed at safeguarding financial stability. This includes, for example, supervisory fees for the Financial Supervision Authority (total of EUR 5.9 million last year) and contributions to the Guarantee Fund (EUR 12.5 million last year). In addition, banks will have to make further payments to the Single European Union Resolution Fund, which is estimated to cost the banks five to six million euros a year. Question 2: 'How do you assess the return on equity of Estonian commercial banks compared to other commercial banks in the European Union? Should the state limit the greed of the banks, however? " The economy of several countries in the European Union was hit during the financial crisis and directly affected commercial banks. Economic adjustment in these countries brought down the quality of banks' assets and also kept the rate of return below. This must certainly be borne in mind when selecting the reference base. Estonian commercial banks are strong and well capitalised. This has contributed to the economic recovery of Estonia since the outbreak of the global financial crisis. Unlike the economies of several other European Union countries, the Estonian economy has been growing steadily since 2010 and the volume of the Estonian economy has increased by almost one fifth during this period. This will certainly also enable banks to achieve greater productivity in their assets. In addition, banks operating in Estonia are more cost-effective, allowing for additional productivity. It is important that Estonian people increasingly use electronic channels to make payments, which allows reducing the share of cash in economic transactions and optimising expenditure related to the office network. Bank offices can focus more on providing advisory services. In particular, effective competition is a factor influencing the profitability of banks. In Estonia, 16 banks, including branches of foreign credit institutions, offer their services, from which consumers can choose. Question three: '2000. The Reform Party has called Estonia a great success story. Why has this system had so few followers in Europe that it is so good and business-friendly? ' For historical reasons, the Estonian economy lags behind the amount of capital used per employee from countries with higher income levels. However, the more capital per employee, the more productive the economy is. The Estonian income tax system favours investment activity and the accumulation of capital, which has contributed to productivity growth and an increase in our standard of living. Estonia stands out among other European Union countries as having a high share of investment in GDP. From 2000 onwards, the share of investment in GDP has been the highest in the European Union. An example of this is the influx of foreign direct investment per employee, in which Estonia is among the leaders of Central and Eastern Europe. In the light of the poor economic growth of recent years, many European countries need to reform to improve their competitiveness. These reforms will certainly include, on the one hand, a reduction in expenditure and, on the other hand, a reduction in the tax burden, especially in those countries where the role of the public sector in the economy has widened and business sector taxes have proved burdensome to the economy. Tax policy is usually conservative and these changes do not occur overnight. Reforms of tax systems are long-term and complex processes. Question 4: 'According to the Minister for Finance, the government coalition, as one of the sources of cover, discussed the restoration of the classic corporate tax instead of raising excise duties on fuel. What were the arguments that led the cabinet to choose to increase excise duties on fuel? " The sharp rise in the corporate tax burden would reduce the growth prospects of the Estonian economy and thus reduce the opportunities for increasing the incomes of Estonian people. This is one of the many reasons why the government does not consider it right to suddenly increase the corporate tax burden. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U159	Please, Prime Minister Taavi Kõivas!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U160	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! First of all, I would like to thank you for this very important theme and for the opportunity to present the positions of the government in Parliament on this subject as well. I agree with the inquisitors that this issue is still topical. Although gas consumption in Estonia is decreasing and is not comparable to electricity consumption in terms of security, for example, it is important that there is security of gas supply in Estonia. The government has taken several steps to increase this security of supply in Estonia. But let's go to questioning questions and answers. The first question: 'What is the current state of the Finnish application and readiness for the construction of a regional terminal? You have admitted that Estonia would be better suited to building a regional terminal. Why was Finland still chosen? ' Under the agreement concluded last November, Finland will have the opportunity to submit an application for funding the work of the regional terminal from the Connecting Europe Facility until the end of next year. Just like you said. If by this time the Finnish developer has not submitted the application, Estonia will have the opportunity to consider submitting the application. The agreement was based on the fact that connecting isolated markets to a larger volume gas market is a priority for the development of the Estonian gas market. It is therefore our priority to establish the Balticconnector connection between Estonia and Finland. A gas market of sufficient capacity is necessary precisely to allow the emergence of a sustainable LNG terminal. It is certainly not conceivable that the Estonian market alone would be sufficient to serve such a large terminal. We know that a LNG terminal has been established in Lithuania. By the way, Estonia also consumes nearly a quarter of the gas that comes from Lithuania via LNG terminal. In order to be able to build either an LNG terminal of similar size to Estonia or Finland, it is very important that the Estonian and Finnish markets are connected. This objective is directly in line with the European Commission's policy of combining markets and not creating isolated areas called energy islands in the European slang. The second question: 'The construction of the Balticconnector is justified by the need to increase energy security. How will Balticconnector increase Estonia's energy security if gas imported from Russia is also used in Finland? ' As I explained in the previous reply, the Balticconnector must be seen first and foremost as an opportunity to connect the Baltic and Finnish gas markets. Despite the fact that Balticconnector does not independently add additional sources of supply, it adds a new supply channel and therefore increases security of supply in the event of infrastructure disruptions. A similar market coupling project is also underway between Lithuania and Poland, the project is called GIPL, or Gas Interconnection Poland, Lithuania. All these efforts serve the same purpose: from Finland to Poland, the combined market is more attractive for investments, including LNG terminal investments, improves gas supply security, creates price competition, and creates the possibility to use jointly previously developed supply solutions in the event of supply difficulties. This means that we are not alone in the event of problems. The dependence of the Finnish gas market on imports of Russian gas alone is in no way a factor that enhances Estonia's security. The Balticconnector is therefore a project that creates new value and opportunities for the entire region. Question 3: 'The Finnish gas company Gasum has a long-term gas supply contract with Gazprom, which is valid until the end of 2025. In recent years the price of gas supplied in Finland has been consistently lower than in Estonia. Why should Finland be interested in building Balticconnector at all if it brings a more expensive price to the Finnish people, because Gazprom is not interested in selling cheap gas through the Finnish gas company? ' Finland has confirmed at all levels that it is interested in the Balticconnector project for these reasons. This was also agreed between the Prime Ministers last November. The exact terms of the Gazprom and Gasum agreements cannot be commented in detail as Prime Minister of Estonia, but on the basis of public sources, I can say that the Finnish gas company Gasum and Gazprom have now reached Stockholm arbitration on the price issue with their differences. Such negotiations would certainly be easier in a situation where the Community's rest of Europe's gas system would also allow competing offers from other suppliers. The Balticconnector, together with the Lithuanian LNG terminal and the Polish-Lithuanian connections, would also create such price arbitrage. Finland does not need to look far for a positive example. The share of gas supplied from Lithuania in the Estonian gas market has reached a quarter already. There is competition on the market, but it has to be acknowledged that there is still a long way to go, and there is a great deal to be won by Finland, just as there is a lot to be won by Estonia. Question 4: "What is the current world market price of LNG compared to the price of pipeline gas sold by Gasum in Finland? ' The world market price of LNG has been statistically higher than the price of pipe gas imported into Finland. However, LNG prices are volatile, and in circumstances where there is an oversupply of LNG production and the US is entering the market in the coming years, it may periodically be cheaper than the pipeline gas related to the price of other fuels from Russia. Currently, the Finnish gas exchange price and northwest European LNG prices are nearly equal. Question 5: 'The establishment of the Balticconnector will cover 75% of the Connecting Europe Facility and the remaining 25% will be distributed equally between Estonia and Finland. Is it true that there was also a possibility on the table that the costs would be shared between Estonia and Finland according to the gas demand of the countries? Why did you give up such a useful option for Estonia? 'The burden-sharing will ultimately be decided by national energy market regulators and, inter alia, the geographical distribution of the socio-economic costs and benefits of the infrastructure project will be taken into account. In Estonia, the power market regulator is known to be the Competition Authority. Question 6: 'Estonia's contribution to Balticconnector is about EUR 25 million. What effect does the construction of Balticconnector have on transmission charges? How much is the cost of gas network charges for consumers? " If the assumptions put forward by the questioners were to be taken into account, this would mean an increase in the network tariff of around 45%, but in the final tariff, if the consumption volumes for 2014 were to be taken as a basis, this would represent a share of 1,5% of the gas price. It can also be taken as a basis that the positive impact on gas prices would be considerably higher because, if you look at the 2009-2014 period, the average gas exchange price in Finland was about five euros per megawatt-hour, i.e. 10% cheaper than the price of gas consumed in Estonia. So this 1.5% increase in the final tariff is still a rather small price compared to the potentially lower gas price as a result of the merger of markets. Of course, there is also a very strong security aspect here. This 1.5% of the price of gas also brings us that no one will be able to use gas supplies against Estonia as a political weapon. I'm sure it's worth something too. Let's say, as a matter of fact, we've already been through this with electricity. Thanks to the fact that there are two very high-quality electricity connections between Estonia and Finland - EstLink 1 and EstLink 2 with a total capacity of 1000 megawatts - the price of electricity in Estonia is considerably cheaper than would have been without these connections. This also gives us a share of Nordic energy. Even if, because of their infrainvestments in Estonia, network charges increased, this increase is very much lower than the positive effect of the fact that the price of electricity is now much more favourable. Latvia and Lithuania have not yet opened their gas markets and electricity is considerably more expensive there than, for example, Estonia or Finland. This parallel, I think, also helps to explain a little why, when making an investment, in fact, the end result is a more favourable price, not to mention that security of supply is much greater than without these connections. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U167	Andres Ammas, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U168	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Prime Minister! There was a brief discussion of security considerations in your answers. This motivates me to ask whether I understood you correctly that there is currently no strategic gas supply on Estonia's territory. If so, is it planned to be created and when?
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U169	Thank you. To put it this way, our strategic gas supply is when we have a number of alternative connections and it is possible to obtain gas from different sources of supply that are independent of each other. At present, the gas that Estonia consumes is stored in the Inckalns repository in the territory of the Republic of Latvia, and it is theoretically possible that if something happens with a high-speed connection, Estonia will not really get it. At the same time, in terms of security considerations, I consider Latvia to be a country in a very similar security environment to Estonia, and I believe that this gas supply in Latvia will certainly serve our interests in the future. But yes, indeed, the theoretical possibility is that if anything should happen to the connection, there is this potential problem. The other way around, nor can electricity be stored in batteries as a strategic security reserve. Our strategic security measure is precisely the electricity connections: the fact that Estonia is connected to the Nordic electricity grid, the fact that there are connections between Estonia and Latvia, the fact that we are also establishing a third power line in Latvia in cooperation with the Latvian Elering equivalent, and, of course, the power of electricity generation, which exists independently in Estonia. For example, Latvia does not have enough capacity to survive or only consume electricity produced in Latvia throughout the year, and therefore Estonia is, in a sense, the guarantor of energy security for Latvia as regards electricity connections.

ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U177	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! I will respond to your inquiry on 10 June on the return of property in the course of the property reform and the payment of compensation to tenants. Question number one: Has the property reform been completed in Estonia? New examples suggest that the return of individual properties to the descendants of former owners is still ongoing. This process is described by the Estonian Tenants' Association as a follow-up to the ownership reform, which seeks to bring the lost fortune back to the market. " The ownership reform, which began at the time of the restoration of independence and culminated in the next decade, has met the objectives set out in the law. Most areas of ownership reform, including privatisation of businesses and housing, agricultural reform and many others, ended more than 10 to 15 years ago. The vast majority of labour-intensive land reform, and mainly the return and compensation of unlawfully disposed assets under it, have also ended. A little over 500 files, or 0.4% of the total, have still been opened from a file of almost 140000 unlawfully disposed assets. They shall contain requirements for which no decision has been taken. The main reason for the cessation of administrative proceedings is the facts relating to the succession, the search for heirs and the proof of the right to succession. Proceedings have also not been closed because of litigation, border disputes, disputes between entitled persons and disputes between local authorities. The law on the basis of which the ownership reform is based allows for an extension of the period until 30 June 2016 and decisions on the compensation of property must be taken by the end of the following year at the latest. Cases relating to legal proceedings or successions may remain in the proceedings for a longer period. A small number of land privatisation operations are also under way and the land will continue to be transferred to municipal property and the land will be left to state ownership. The second question: 'When does the Government of the Republic plan to return the property of displaced persons to Germany, after the annulment of Article 7 (3) of the Oras, cited in the judgments of the Supreme Court, make the necessary new arrangements? The process is currently underway at the level of insufficient competence of officials, in which tenants living in returning homes belonging to displaced persons cannot obtain specific answers from the Commission to their questions. The Supreme Court has referred to the need for such a new regime through several judgments. ' The Supreme Court has made a number of decisions on matters relating to § 7 (3) of the ORAS, but late in the judgment of 10 March 2008, the General Assembly of the Supreme Court took the view that the local committees for the return and compensation of unlawfully disposed assets must start to process the statements made in respect of unlawfully disposed assets belonging to German displaced persons in general. As a result of the decision, the balancing of refund procedures does not require any additional regulation, since the instructions required for the functioning of local committees are included in the positions of the Supreme Court General Assembly. To date, nearly all the claims of migrants have been the subject of the necessary decisions, with the exception of some procedures that have stopped due to litigation and personal data protection requirements. Question 3: "Who do you think should check compliance with § 17 (5) of the Property Reform Act? This section, as we know, obliges the return of houses to displaced persons to check whether or not the former owner has received compensation from Germany. ' Pursuant to § 17 (5) of the Property Reform Foundation Act, a person who is a legitimate subject of a property reform does not have the right to claim the return or compensation of unlawfully transferred property in respect of assets that have already been returned or compensated, unless it has been established that the property has been returned or compensated to another person without a legal basis. Compliance with this provision at the various stages of the procedure shall be subject to control by the Regional Committee on the return and compensation of unlawfully disposed assets, by the municipal authorities carrying out the refund or compensation procedure and by the supervising county governor. The possible early compensation of the property by other countries, in particular the Soviet Union, is controlled by the Regional Commission at the initial stage of the procedure. Question 4: 'Until then, only the City Government of Tallinn has checked the compensation and received reports from the German archives in a number of cases that, in the case of houses that have already been returned to former owners, this has also been a payment of compensation by the German Government. Therefore, the return has not been in accordance with the law. After this judgment, how is it ensured that the return of the assets returned to the migrants complies with Article 17 (5) of the ORAS will be reviewed throughout Estonia and not only in Harjumaa? As we know, assets have been returned to residents in Narva, Pärnu, Tartu, Saaremaa, Viljandi County and elsewhere. ' In order to determine the compensation paid to the so-called after-resettlements pursuant to the Soviet-German Treaty of 1941, the Ministry of Finance submitted a number of aggregated enquiries to the Federal Republic of Germany concerning persons who, according to the documents found in the archives, may have left Estonia on the basis of that agreement and who or whose heirs have submitted claims for the return or compensation of property. The lists drawn up in counties and municipalities contained a total of 242 such persons. For any of the persons listed in the request, the German State Archives did not provide information on the payment of the contractual compensation or the transfer of the replacement assets. According to the relevant legislation and the correspondence of German officials, the replacement assets may have been used by migrants, but they were to be transferred to their property after the expected end of the war for this party. The persons qualified as refugees were not entitled to compensation for the abandoned assets or replacement assets. However, in a number of later cases, the Court held that compensation for solidarity granted to displaced persons and persons arriving as refugees in the Federal Republic of Germany in the post-war period should also be regarded as compensation for the restoration of property. As a result of the judgment, the county committees for the return and compensation of unlawfully disposed assets, as well as the Tallinn City Government, began to submit inquiries to the German Equalisation Payments Archives for the purpose of establishing the payment of solidarity benefits. Question 5: "Are you in agreement with the position of the Union of Estonian Tenants that in the 1990s forced tenants were harmed when their home was taken away and the state gave nothing to replace them? The tenants were essentially raised to the street and had to acquire new housing at market price for their money. While residents of apartment buildings could privatise their apartments. ' I will answer this question and the next question together. I will also read the sixth question: 'When does the government intend to deal with the issue of compensation for injustice and material damage suffered by the families of tenants who have lost their former home in the course of a property reform? As your Prime Minister, has it not occurred to you that you could apologise to the forced tenants on behalf of the Estonian state? This would be followed by a government programme to ensure that the material costs incurred in the course of the injustice are compensated for by the forced tenants. ' Ownership reform is a major structural reform that affected every person in one way or another, which was crucial for Estonia's successful transition from the Soviet social organisation to a free and democratic civil society. We must recognise that the starting position of the various members of our society (Estonian citizens and new residents, urban and rural residents, legitimate and new civil societies) was inevitably different in the process of returning and privatising property. The tenant of the housing to be returned, the owner of the private house, the member of the joint or public holding could not participate in the privatisation of the dwelling on the same terms as most of the residents of the apartment buildings. The latter, in turn, could not privatise the land on just as favorable terms as the owner of the land-based dwelling. Our country had to choose between fast and decisive and therefore simpler solutions and reforms that were finished and balanced to detail, but which required much more time. The first path was chosen. Although, as a former republic of the Soviet Union, we did not start out in the first start line among the liberated socialist countries, Estonia ended the most decisive part of the transition reforms faster than most of our destiny mates. Reform laws were completed and adopted exceptionally quickly compared to today's legislative timeframe. They provided mechanisms to involve as much of society as possible in the reform process and to help mitigate the social repercussions of reforms. Support measures for the resettlement and protection of the rights of residents of returnable rental homes as tenants were also planned. During the initial phase of the transition reforms in 1991-1992, the Government of the Republic handed over almost all the state housing fund to local municipalities, with a view to preparing for the mass privatisation of housing. During the restoration of Estonia's independence, the population of larger cities was expected to decline considerably due to the departure of Soviet troops, officials and their families. The municipalities had to register and privatise the vacant apartments to the tenants of the residential buildings to be returned as a matter of priority. The assessment made in the early 1990's showed that 50%, or some 6,000, of nationalized homes had been preserved by then. This assessment was confirmed by subsequent direct data from local governments and the tenants' association, according to which some 22500 families lived in 5226 returning houses. However, due to the large-scale sale of so-called settlement rights, there were very few vacant apartments left in the cities, especially in Tallinn, and the possibility of offering them to tenants of residential buildings to be returned for privatisation remained largely unrealized. The Act still provides tenants with benefits and the right to buy a new dwelling, funding opportunities for building a dwelling and the right to extend the rental contract for a total period of up to eight years. To the tenants and municipalities of the houses to be returned, the State provided funds for preferential loans, including EEK 507 million, for loans that could be repaid with privatisation bonds, for non-refundable grants as well as for municipal buildings. In 2001-2002 alone, more than 100 million kroons were allocated to the Republic's Government's property reform reserve fund, partly through KredEx, to solve housing problems of tenants living in buildings returned to legitimate owners. The City Government of Tallinn has publicly confirmed that the housing problems of forced tenants in the city have been solved. This seems to be confirmed by the fact that in recent years applications for money have rarely been returned to the needs of tenants of houses. It is possible to compensate for the material damage caused by the State or local government in the course of the ownership reform on the basis of a specific claim for compensation, by proving, in court or out-of-court, the damage and its amount. It is in recent years that a number of long-term property reform disputes have come to an end, resulting in significant compensation by the State to the beneficiaries. Between 2014 and 2015, more than one million euros have been paid in damages from the Government's property reform reserve fund. I understand that the questioners obviously refer not to compensation for damage caused by illegal decisions and transactions during the reform process, but to the legislative revision of the entire reform process and the regularisation of new support or compensation measures. When the tenants' union collects signatures to submit a collective appeal to the Riigikogu, the Riigikogu is competent to deal with the proposals submitted in accordance with the current law. Of course, the Government of the Republic cannot stand by in this debate either, and it will evaluate these proposals. Thank you.
