ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U198	Thank you. Please, Prime Minister Taavi Kõivas!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U199	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen of the House! I would like to thank you for this much-needed raise and for the opportunity to present the government's positions on administrative reform issues and to give an overview of what we have achieved with the preparations to date. I will respond to your inquiry on 10 June. Question 1: 'How do you think the principles of local administration reform carried out by the government coalition are in line with the principles of the European Charter for Local Government? Who of the European experts on local democracy have you involved in preparing for the reform? " In choosing the content and principles of the local government reform, we follow the principles of the nature and governance of the local government and the guarantees of the local government as laid down in the legislation. The local government's ability to manage and organise local life must go hand in hand with rights and obligations, i.e. the municipality must have real capacity and the resources and competence necessary to do this in order to be responsible for the performance of its tasks. Local authorities play a key role in providing people with daily services at local level, but it is important to remember that, apart from providing services, the role of local authorities is to exercise public authority and ensure democracy. Together, these roles serve the purpose of the functioning of the local government, which is the well-being of the population. A number of experts from local government and local democracy, such as Professor Georg Sootla from Tallinn University, Mikk Lõhmus from Tallinn University of Technology and experts from local government associations with long-term experience, have contributed to the preparation of the reform of local government in the expert committee led by the Minister of State Administration: from the Estonian Cities Union Jüri Võigema and the Estonian Association of Rural Governments Märt Moll, who have experience in participating in the work of the Committee of the Regions of the European Union and also in the work of the Congress of Local and Regional Municipal Governments of the Council of Europe. In addition, there are ministerial officials involved in the preparation of the reform, including those with long-term experience of participation in the work of the Council of Europe's Committee on Local and Regional Democracy and the European Committee on Democracy and Governance. The second question: Do and how do you involve local authorities and their alliances in developing the basis for reform, the criteria? What views and opinions have you received from them? 'The representatives of the national self-government associations are involved in the work of both the expert panel on local government reform and the government committee headed by the Prime Minister. During the summer, meetings were held in eight counties with county elders, county councils and local government leaders, where officials of the Ministry of Interior introduced the timetable and principles of local government reform. The leaders of both national and county self-government associations have given wide-ranging feedback. The need for reform is widely perceived in every region of Estonia. Feedback and proposals have largely touched on the process and stages of the reform. Much of the proposals address the criteria underpinning the reform and possible new challenges to be met after the reform. The third question: 'What are the government's plans on the matter of county administration? Do you intend and how you intend to increase the role of municipalities and their county associations in the decision-making process concerning the comprehensive development of the county? Are and what functions will rural governments be left to perform? ' In the first phase of the administrative reform to implement the reform of local government by 2018, the Action Plan of the Government Union has agreed to reorganize this part of the tasks of rural governments, which includes modernising the supervision of local governments and harmonising the divisional and territorial management of the county. The Government's revised work plan has been agreed as follows: In order to strengthen the cooperation of municipalities in the performance of their tasks at regional level, an integrated body allowing horizontal coordination in each county will be set up. The aim is to transfer self-government tasks from rural governments to joint institutions or, in the case of sufficient size and capacity, also directly to municipalities. In the preparation of changes in these areas and further discussions, the principle is that the municipal functions of rural governments, the nature of which allows them, are transferred to local governments or the cooperation body of local authorities and the organisation of the supervision of the county government is reorganized in order to carry out the functions of public authorities, in particular the functions of administrative and state supervision. Discussions have taken place in expert panels, but decisions to make concrete proposals to the government committee have not yet been taken. Question 4: Which areas of public administration do you intend to focus most on? To what extent does the public sector's personnel section concern local authorities? ' The government's aim is to make the government sector more efficient. In a context of declining employment, it is our desire to avoid a shortage of hands in the private sector due to the public sector in order to support the long-term sustainability of the economy. In order to ensure that the maintenance of the general government does not become a greater burden on the private sector, the reduction in the working-age population also requires a reduction in the number of employees in the general government by an average of 1% or 700-800 employees per year. At the same time, due to increased labour competition and competitive wage pressures, the government's objective is to raise government employees' wages in line with the general increase in productivity in society. These objectives can only be achieved on a sustainable basis through fundamental reforms in the activities of the sectors and in the organisation of the institutions. The contribution of all ministries to achieving the objectives is very important. A general austerity trend of 1% per year on average could also be followed by the local government sector, but the government cannot make mandatory provision for local authorities in which areas to reduce the number of employees and labour costs, as this would undermine the local government's right of self-regulation. Activities and areas for saving must be analysed by local authorities themselves and, where possible, savings must also be applied. For example, it is possible to consolidate support services, use common public procurement or also create IT environments. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U296	Thank you. I now ask the Minister of Rural Affairs of the Riigikogu to answer the question, which bears the number 65!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U297	Honourable President! Honourable members and guests of the Riigikogu! The first question: 'How much of Estonia's territory is now covered by African swine fever? What has the government done to control the disease and to prevent the plague from reaching domestic pigs? " Since September 2014, when the first African swine fever was detected in Estonia, it has been detected in wild boars in 11 counties and in domestic pigs in seven counties. If we look at disease-related activities, we can group them as follows. First of all, the development of a common strategy and Baltic cooperation. As early as July 2014, agreements on the disease control strategy were reached between the European Commission, the Baltic States and Poland, and this strategy has been updated following the recommendations of the European Food Safety Authority (EFSA) in August this year. On 13 August, the ministers of agriculture in the Baltic States and Poland sent a joint letter to the European Union Commissioner for Health and Food Safety, Andriukaitis, with a detailed proposal to combat African swine fever. It was the intention of the proposers, among other things, to achieve greater flexibility in trade in live pigs and animal products between the same zones in other Member States. This work is not over and will continue in the coming months. But yes, as the good questioner pointed out, certain trade restrictions have certainly put the pig farmers in a third restricted zone in a more difficult position. When we are talking about establishing rules and improving control, the whole legal framework for disease control, which contains the Law on epizootic diseases, the Code of Conduct for the Control of African swine fever, contingency plans, has actually been in place for years. The most important measures to limit the spread of the disease from forest to farms are compliance with biosecurity requirements and the reduction of the feral pig population. The strategy specified requirements for both. In September 2014 and also in August this year, the PPP supervisory authorities and authorised veterinarians visited each individual pig farmer to verify compliance with the biosecurity requirements and to issue a recommendation. Speaking about the package of measures, of course, it is about raising awareness among all parties. As early as 2011, leaflets were distributed at border crossing points. They clarified restrictions on personal luggage, including that meat products may not be imported from third countries. These leaflets were also sent to bus and train companies engaged in international transport operations. In 2013, an information leaflet introducing the African swine plague was commissioned and sent to all pig farmers across Estonia via direct mail. We have a website called www.seakatk.ee, where all relevant information is gathered. Enhancing the capacity and cooperation between the state and its partners has also been one of the preventive and monitoring measures. The national disease control authority was set up in September 2014, with a local disease control committee in each county from 2015. As they do their daily work, they constantly negotiate monitoring and other guidelines. And as I have already said from this remote control, this work is clearly more active than it was originally planned. Of course, we have also planned to increase our technical capabilities. One part of this relates, for example, to the acquisition of cold chambers so that hunted boars are not kept until the results of the samples arrive, at the mercy of nature, and both the spread of disease and the deterioration of meat are avoided. Cooperation with scientists is also important. We would very much like researchers at the Estonian University of Life Sciences to analyse every case, and that is what they are doing, and to make recommendations to prevent the spread of the plague. This is not only about Estonia, but we want to share our knowledge with all other European countries, including those where there is no swine plague at the moment. I am thinking of strengthening both biosecurity requirements and compensation for disease damage. So there are a number of measures that have been taken to control the disease. Question 2: 'How much has the Ministry of Agriculture spent on the prevention and notification of African swine fever? ' For practical prevention, EUR 300000 has been orientally spent in the period 2014 to this point. A mobile burner costing EUR 227000 has also been acquired. In 2013 information leaflets were commissioned to distribute to all Estonian pig farmers. The brochures have also been translated into Russian and smaller prints have been used to provide English-language information. After all, foreign hunters also hunt in the Estonian forest. 'Do you think that the so-called red or restricted zones in Estonia are reasonably established? How does the government intend to compensate pig farms located in the restricted zone III for economic losses, given that no African swine fever has been found on these farms? " How are we going to do that? Firstly, as I referred to, we would like to get more clarity from the European Commission on the more specific reasons for establishing this zone and for how long this zone may be in force. Today we can say that we are somewhat unaware of the conditions under which the zone will cease to exist and the restriction will be lifted. That's one. The question also concerned the economic cost of the African swine fever. In an outbreak, or farm where pigs are destroyed, 100% of the damage is compensated and includes feed that is forced to be destroyed. We have also proposed to the European Commission that an exceptional market organisation measure be opened to allow aid to be applied for to pig farmers in the third zone who are forced to sell their produce much cheaper than before the disease. 'How do you predict the economic damage that African swine fever could cause to cereal farmers? ' If we look at the world of grain just because it depends very much on the stocks of cereals, the quality of the grain, the size of the crops, the weather forecasts and so on, it can now be said that there is no impact of classical swine fever on the price of cereals and its export. If we look at how much our cereal growers are selling to pig farmers, we must say that these quantities are not very large. The growing of grain in Estonia as animal feed is within the limit of 500000 tonnes, while all grain production has undergone a pretty good increase and it could be said that in a good year, our grain production will triple the feed demand. Our most important countries of export of cereals are Saudi Arabia, Germany, Kenya, Latvia, Norway and Algeria. As regards the direct impact of the plague, experts at the Estonian University of Life Sciences estimate that the infected fruit is not dangerous if it has been dried or stored for at least 30 days in such a way that it has not been in contact with the source of infection, either additionally or not at all. At the same time, it is important to make sure that no one tries to exploit the situation of our country in competitive conditions and to refer to the swine plague as an argument preventing the sale of Estonian grain. The fifth question: 'Ago Pärtel, the head of the Food and Veterinary Office, has not been able to take decisions proactively in time and has rather disinformed society. What position does the Minister for Rural Affairs have with regard to the employment relationship of Mr Ago Pärtel, Director General of the Food and Veterinary Office? ' I have said to the public and from this remote control that, as far as crisis management is concerned, the legislator has placed a very high level of direct responsibility on the Director-General of the Food and Veterinary Office. My greatest concern has been to inform the public, to inform it transparently and honestly. The case of Vireen has also been through the hall and the public, and because of the fake billing and the public wrestling, Vireen's former leader has resigned. The task of eradicating pigs infected by African swine fever is shared between several agencies and, on the basis of the information received yesterday, we have initiated disciplinary proceedings against Ago Pärtel. Once this investigation is over, we can inform the public in more detail.

ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U375	Mr President! Ladies and gentlemen, In order to understand the content of the question, I will begin Napoleon's time in France. At that time, the first forensicsman acted there, according to our understanding. If my memory isn't fooled, his name was Joseph Fouché, a former criminal and a very effective fighter with crime, only he had trouble identifying the criminals. His method looked like this, about the size of our hall, but in the middle of a round room, spies sat around and criminals walked around, and for hours the spies tried to remember the faces of the criminals. Clearly, such a system could not be effective. Progress: After a few decades, a man named Bertillon appeared, a great and very famous archaeologist, I don't remember now, either son or grandson, and he figured out a method that was called bertillionage. He was convinced that if you take certain places on the human body and measure the differences between them, the sequence of proportions is original for every human being. A young man, a little over 20 years of trouble, but then it turned out that criminals were really identifiable that way. But the system was very complicated and it was difficult to use, because it was done in a melancholy and measured as the left leg wished, and often failed to achieve anything. And once again, progress. Two men who came from England and worked in colonies did not distinguish local people face by face, they were then Indians or Chinese, and suddenly they discovered that fingerprints would never happen again. Two men, regardless of each other, made this discovery and offered a dactyloscopy. After that, it has proved itself all over the world. However, problems began to arise in the 1930's when the men of Chicago and New York began to try to get rid of the fingerprints. For example, sandpaper tried to rub down these lines and to get rid of them by burning them, even trying to replant the skin from its ass. But it turns out it's not helping anything. When the skin has grown into a new place, he'll change his structure and the fingerprints will come back. So from the 1930s, the entire civilized world knows that in life, from about 18 days to death, the fingerprints won't change. Our Home Office doesn't know that. If you complete your passport, you will be fingerprinted. And if you're going to file any other document in two years, you're going to have to print it again. ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U376	Mr Pevkur, Minister of the Interior, will answer the question. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U377	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Thank you to the interviewer for such a nice fictional introduction to such a small problem, as he himself rightly said! Let's go through these questions quickly. The first question was that if a person's fingerprints do not change, what is the reason for the requirement to redefine every two years? Fingerprints on the document chip shall be occupied when submitting an application for a travel document and a residence card. I would like to stress that it is precisely when the application is made. When issuing the document, fingerprints will be taken again, with the aim of comparing biometric data. The fingerprints on the chip of the document shall be compared with the fingerprints of the applicant. In electronic identification of electronic documents, it is essential that biometric data are of the highest quality and reliable possible. As the data age, their reliability decreases. Also, over time, the technology for capturing and storing and processing fingerprints will become more accurate and high-quality, as one important paper says. Thus, when two reasons are raised, it can be said that the need for a person to be dactyloscopy is due to the fact that the person has been identified unambiguously and seriously. Secondly, the fingerprints taken from the applicant must be technically processed and be of good quality for identification purposes throughout the period of validity of the document. Speaking of quality, the inquisitor correctly stated that the fingerprint would not change. But the quality of the fingerprint can change and it can affect the unique papilla arc. For example, it may take time or be damaged. If you cut your finger, the fingerprint has changed, and it is also possible to deform it due to the disease, etc. If the finger gets between the two stones, the print changes, there's nothing to do. If the quality of the fingerprints has changed, it may also reduce the likelihood of a hit in a future comparison from the chip of the document. I would also like to say that in 2004, the European Union has laid down the obligation to use biometric data, including fingerprints, in the documents of the citizens of the Member States. When asked why we are using them or why we are occupying them again, it must be taken into account that biometric documents allow individuals to use e-gates, for example, or ABC gates, where biometric data are removed from the document chip and compared with data put in the person's scanner. If these data are not fresh, a person may have a problem. He can't get through the ABC Gate and direct a routine check. And then we're all upset why the ABC gate doesn't work. Doesn't work because the prints aren't high enough. The second question of the inquiry is what interferes the Government of the Republic with the initiation of an amendment to the law with a view to abolishing the requirement of repeated dactyloscopy. I understand that this is still two years, and I must point out that people do not have to go to the Police and Border Guard Board every two years to give new fingerprints. They are usually taken again when a person goes to apply for a new document at the end of the validity of the passport or residence card. We know that the term of validity of the passport is five years and the members of the Riigikogu have been proposed that it should be ten years in the future. I've been supporting it for ten years. The validity of the residence card is linked to a specific person, up to a maximum of five years. If a person's personal or residence card is stolen or destroyed before the expiry date and a person is therefore going to apply for a new document, he or she will not need to be fingerprinted if less than two years have elapsed since the previous dactyloscopy.

ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U387	Ladies and gentlemen, Minister, thank you very much. In December 2014, ministers of the European Union adopted Council conclusions on 'Migrations in European Union development cooperation'. The conclusions highlighted the need to help partner countries increase their capacity to deal with migrants and to contribute to raising awareness on migration-related issues: return, criminal networks, human trafficking, human rights. The positive aspects of migration were highlighted, as many emigrants are helping to return knowledge, technology and even resources to their homeland. They also contribute to the development of the country of destination, for example by setting up businesses. In May this year, the European Commission published a Communication entitled "A European Agenda on Migration', which addresses both the root causes of migration and the fight against poverty and the strengthening of measures to prevent irregular migration. The Communication also addresses support for legal and controlled migration. The Estonian Government must be guided by the positions of our country in December 2014 in the Cabinet of Government and the same month in the Foreign Affairs Committee of the Riigikogu at the December meeting of the Ministers of Development of the Foreign Affairs Council of the European Union. Our inquiry has been completed on 10 June. Today is September 21st and situations have changed greatly, but some questions remain. 'Is Estonia prepared to coordinate more effectively between the sectoral structures of the European Union, as well as between the Member States' own policies, to acknowledge and implement development coherence? Can Estonia contribute to creating decent living conditions in the main countries of origin of emigration? " And thirdly, " What can we do to increase the security we consider to be the main cause of migration in the countries of origin? " We hope for exhaustive answers, and we will certainly ask questions, given the state of things today. Thank you!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U388	Mr Pevkur, Minister of the Interior, will answer the question. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U389	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Good heirs! I am happy to discuss migration with you today and it seems that many times in the near future. In particular, these questions, if the inquisitors allow us to refer to them, most of their content falls within the competence of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. But we have a whole government, in cooperation with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, we were able to review the issues and we tried to write everything down nicely to answer the questioners. The first question is whether Estonia is prepared to coordinate more effectively between the sectoral structures of the European Union, as well as between the policies of the Member States themselves, to acknowledge and implement development coherence. We have a nice concept, policy coordination for development, which has been shortened to PCD and translated into Estonian as 'coherence of policies in development cooperation'. I'm sure the representative of the inquisitives knows that very well. And he also knows that all the countries of the European Union, including Estonia, are bound by a PCD agreement. According to this, other policies should support the achievement of development cooperation objectives, and it is important that policies with a major international impact do not nullify joint contributions to development aid. A broader awareness and more effective implementation of the principles of policy coherence is also one of the clear priorities of the current Luxembourg Presidency of the European Union and we are very pleased that this is one of their priorities in the area of development cooperation. Certainly, Estonia supports the Presidency and is also guided by this principle in planning its development cooperation. If we look at our development cooperation and humanitarian aid agenda up to 2020, it will be in there nicely. The second question: 'Can Estonia contribute to creating decent living conditions in the main countries of origin of emigration? ' Now I'm going to say a little more, but the subject is burning. By way of introduction, it is certainly in our interest to support all measures that would help manage migration already in the region of origin, and we have done so as far as we can. Estonia is the sixth largest contributor to Frontex missions. I think it went through today to answer a question, but let's say it again. Last year we participated in Frontex operations with a total of 85 police and border guards. When it was put on human working days, we were on Frontex missions nearly 3,000, namely 2985 working days. And if you look at what else we've been betting on besides human power, we had a fast boat, a plane, and so on, a smaller technique on Frontex missions. This year we are planning to participate at least in the same volume. At the moment, it seems that this volume has already been exceeded, so we have actually started to increase our volume. Following the European Commission's proposal to triple the budget for joint operations Triton and Poseidon, we also expressed our willingness to channel additional resources into these joint operations, both in terms of people and technology. We also support the strengthening of the role of Frontex in organising joint readmission operations. Now that we come to military operations, there is currently a military or anti-crime maritime operation, EUNAVFOR Med, in the Mediterranean, whose main objective is to prevent illegal smuggling. Estonia's contribution is the participation of one expert. Estonia has contributed to Africa through development cooperation, humanitarian aid and participation in missions. As we know, Estonia participated in the European Union military mission in the Central African Republic, the training mission and the United Nations mission in Mali. We have given humanitarian aid to the Central African Republic, Mali, South Sudan, Sierra Leone, Guinea, Liberia, Nigeria, Somalia, Chad, Niger and the Democratic Republic of Congo. We have done so in the form of humanitarian aid, mainly through the World Health Organisation (WHO), as well as through UNICEF. As a member of the European Union, we make contributions to the European Development Fund, the bulk of which is directed specifically to support Africa's development. Between 2011 and 2015, Estonia's contribution to the European Development Fund has been around 7.4 million euros. Looking at the contribution that Estonia has made to mitigating the situation in Syria, Jordan, Lebanon, Iraq and Turkey have supported activities there with EUR 1.6 million since the Arab Spring 2011, both through bilateral development cooperation and through the UN humanitarian aid agencies. Experts from the Rescue Team have built a refugee camp in Jordan and participated in UNDAC mission in the Syrian-Turkey border regions of the United Nations crisis assessment and coordination team. NGO Mondo volunteers have done English language and IT training in Jordan, as well as helping civilians in the Za-atari and Azraq refugee camps. I would like to speak separately in Ukraine. Ukraine has, as we know, been one of Estonia's main countries of development cooperation for more than ten years. We will continue to support Ukraine's development in the future by providing both reform experiences and contributions at bilateral level. We are also helping at a multilateral level through programmes and cooperation. Of course, we are always prepared to send additional experts to Ukraine to build up the system there. As far as the financial dimension is concerned, last year assistance to Ukraine amounted to approximately 10% of the total humanitarian aid budget of the Ministry of Foreign Affairs. This amount was EUR 1,2 million. This year's development cooperation from humanitarian aid instruments has provided €2.35 million for Ukraine, including 1.1 million for development cooperation and 1.5 million for humanitarian aid. In addition, we have smaller programmes such as allowing students from Ukraine to study in Estonia through the separation of scholarships and the activities of the Eastern Partnership Centre in Ukraine. Since the beginning of last year, Estonia has allocated a total of 1.76 million euros as humanitarian aid to help victims of the conflict in Ukraine and to alleviate the situation of internal refugees. The aid is mainly targeted through international organisations. In addition, both food and medical assistance has been sent and supported by three Estonian non-profit associations, they are Mondo, Estonian Refugees and Ukrainian Cultural Centre. I'll say one more thing, though. When we look at the OSCE's special missions, Ukraine's mission has contributed 12 people last year, and there are currently seven observers on the OSCE's missions. The financial contribution to the OSCE missions shall be EUR 90000. The third question: 'What can we do to increase the security that we consider to be the main cause of migration in the countries of origin? ' Isn't the main action taken in international cooperation and through NATO and European Union operations, both in Afghanistan, Africa, the Middle East, the Balkans and, of course, in the wider Mediterranean region. I'm sure the heirs know our training mission in Mali, Somalia and the Central African Republic. Civilian missions are also known, primarily to Ukraine and Georgia, but also to Afghanistan, Mali, Niger and the Horn of Africa region. I have already mentioned the mission called EUNAVFOR Med, in which our staff officer participates. And we will add Mali, where the European Union and the United Nations missions have a total of 18 of our active troops. Finally, I refer to the agreement between the Estonian and US Foreign Minister, according to which we will contribute USD 500000 each year to the assistance of Afghanistan, of which USD 300000 will go to support the Afghan police and USD 200000 to support the Afghan armed forces. These are the real bets we've made, but right now I'm just sticking to the focus of the questions. As far as I know, we have a chance to talk more about migration. Today, the Committee on European Union Affairs discussed the proposal that the topic of migration could be discussed in a large Chamber as a matter of national importance. Then we will now take this most burning issue on a separate table in this Chamber and discuss it at length.

ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U396	Honourable, I would remind you that a member of the Riigikogu has the right to one question. Andres Ammas, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U397	Thank you, Mr President-in-Office. Dear Minister, First of all, I would like to take this opportunity to thank you and your assistants for their thorough answers to the written questions of the Free Party Group. My question concerns the possible selection of refugees arriving to us. ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U398	One principle has been agreed internationally. When it comes to the resettlement of people, which has so far taken place in the framework of 'traditional international cooperation', i.e. in coordination with the UN Commissioner for Refugees, the relocation mechanism that would apply within the European Union has not been used at all. In particular, international practice has been taken into account. One important rule is taken into account there, and this important rule is that people are relocated according to which country of destination they are most likely to adapt to. And this greatest probability of adaptation includes cultural, religious, and linguistic backgrounds. This is a principle which is also accepted by the UN Commissioner for Refugees. Clearly, if we have the most common religion here, Christianity, and Christianity, in turn, Orthodox and Lutheranism, we have much less Catholics and Muslims even less, then it must certainly be taken into account. Our constitution, of course, prohibits discrimination by religion, race, gender or nationality, but in view of how people adapt, it is clear that religious background in one country or another is important.

ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U436	The following question has been submitted by Olga Ivanova, Valeri Korb, Tarmo Tamm, Lauri Laas, Mikhail Stalnukhin, Kersti Sarapuu and Märt Sults and it deals with the compliance of security measures of the US embassy with Estonian law. Olga Ivanova, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U437	Mr President-in-Office of the Council. Minister, thank you very much. In May this year, news was published in the press that referred to surveillance activities carried out by the U.S. Embassy and coordinated with the Ministry of the Interior in Tallinn. According to the experts' preliminary opinion, according to the information published in Postimees on 29 May, the surveillance unit of the US Embassy was probably involved in surveillance activities, the compliance of which with Estonian law was called into question by the press. Experts estimate that monitoring people on the streets at random and keeping an eye on the surrounding embassy are on the one hand in accordance with Estonian law. On the other hand, it is questionable if a unit collects and processes data on a so-called suspicious person in a covert manner, as the 'Actual Camera' did. According to the Postimees article mentioned above, if a person had been on a suspicious list, he was photographed and filmed, and a "logbook" began to be kept about his moves. Attorney Mare Tannberg, a professor of penal law at the University of Tartu, admitted that in such a situation, the question may arise as to whether this activity is legitimate or not. 'If a person monitors another person for the purpose of collecting data, this is a private pursuit,' he added. As a result, we have the following questions. First: Who supervises the processing of personal data collected by the U.S. embassy? ' Two: 'Is personal data processed by the US embassy subject to the legal order established in Estonia, i.e. does the US embassy that monitored the person have to inform the Estonian citizen that he has been monitored? ' And the third question: 'Please state and explain whether there is another institution or organisation in Estonia that has been granted such a tracking permit? ' Thank you!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U438	Mr Pevkur, Minister of the Interior, will answer the question. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U439	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Good heirs! I will try to respond quickly and as concretely as possible. The first question is who supervises the processing of personal data collected by the US embassy. I'd like to shift the focus a little bit, put it in the right direction. Your introduction did say it wasn't a surveillance activity. The main focus was on whether Estonia has provided information to the US embassy that would enable the identity of the person to be identified and whether there are any related personal data. In this regard, I can confirm, as in previous speeches on this subject, that, on the basis of the information and verified data collected during the analysis carried out, there is no reason to claim that the parties' activities have gone beyond the limits described in the memorandum. In other words, the activities of the US embassy in ensuring the security of the object are in full compliance with Estonian law and data has not been transmitted to the United States in this form. What has been transmitted is, figuratively speaking, yes- and no-answers. So if the United States embassy has asked for a vehicle to be checked, it has been answered if this vehicle has been suspicious. These have been yes- or no-answers and personal data or details have not been communicated to the United States embassy. The second question: will the personal data processed by the U.S. Embassy be subject to the legal regime established in Estonia, i.e. should the person be informed? As I said, this has not been a surveillance activity. At this point, I would remind you that, in accordance with the Vienna Convention, Estonia has an obligation to ensure the security of foreign representations located here, regardless of whether they are an embassy of the United States, the Russian Federation or the Republic of Finland. As a result, we have worked and will certainly continue to work with embassies to ensure their safety. Data on Estonian citizens have not been transmitted by the Estonian authorities. In case of suspicion of a particular situation-specific threat, we have informed the embassy whether or not there is a threat. The third question: is there any other institution or organisation in Estonia that has been granted such a tracking permit? Once again, no surveillance clearance has been issued to the United States embassy. The embassy identifies possible risks to protect the embassy and its employees, and if there is a threat, Estonia will inform the competent authorities. A code of conduct has also been agreed between the embassy and the Republic of Estonia as to how quickly and when Estonian law enforcement authorities will react to one or another event. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U484	Honourable Riigikogu! Let it be said to begin by saying that the answer to an inquiry is based on how the Riigikogu has established the rules. I basically didn't rest in the summer, I don't have a problem going to the Riigikogu when I have to. I am happy to present the process of administrative reform. The first question: 'In the 100-day plan of the government, you have foreseen that by 14 May the coordination of administrative reform will be placed under the Prime Minister. Has the task been carried out and from this point on, what is the plan for further addressing the administrative reform? What is your role in this? 'The Government of the Republic formed May 14th. a In order to prepare the administrative reform of the government committee, which includes not only the Prime Minister and the Minister of State Administration but also the representative of the Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry, the representative of the Estonian Union of Cities, the representative of the Estonian Association of Rural Governments, the Minister of Education and Research, the Minister of Justice, the Prime Minister's Adviser, the Minister of Finance, the representative of the Parliament's Local Government and Regional Policy Support Group, the representative of the Committee on Rural Affairs of the Riigikogu and two representatives of the Constitutional Commission, as well as the Minister of Interior, the Minister of Health and Labour and the representative of the Office of the President of the Republic. The Commission has met once this summer, and the timetable and principles of local government reform were discussed in the government office on 2 July 2015. In addition to the representatives of the Ministry of Finance, the Ministry of Education and Research and the Ministry of Social Affairs, the representative of the Association of Estonian Cities, the representative of the Association of Estonian Rural Governments and the representative of the Estonian Cooperation Council, Rivo Noorkõiv from Geomedia, Veiko Sepp from the University of Tartu, Georg Sootna from Tallinn University, Mikk Lõhmus from Tallinn University of Technology, Rein Ahas from the University of Tartu, Airi Mikli from the National Audit Office, the Mayor of Rakvere Mihkel Juhkami, a former member of the Riigikogu and the former member of the Western County Elder Neeme Suur, a member of the Riigikogu and the State Law Expert Rait Maruste, Praxis from the State Office Katrin Pihor and the Strategy Director. The panel has met five times. The discussions so far have focused in particular on the timetable and process of carrying out the reform of local government and on the criteria underlying the reform. The next meeting is this Friday. The role of the Minister of State Administration in the process is to guide the development of the concept, principles and draft law of administrative reform and the process of implementation of the reform, as well as to support the assessment of compliance by local authorities and the conduct of voluntary mergers. You are certainly aware of this, but broadly, the reform is being carried out in three stages. The first includes the drafting of the necessary draft legislation for the reform. The law can, of course, be passed by the Riigikogu itself. During this year, the government plans to write draft laws, send them to coordination and send them to the Riigikogu at the latest on the anniversary of the Republic, so that the Riigikogu can then pass the laws by Midsummer Day if it considers it necessary. The second stage is the implementation of the voluntary reform and the preparation of accessions, which will mainly start in the summer-Autumn next year and will lead to the 2017 local elections. I hope that this own-initiative phase will be very active, as you know, we will also motivate it financially and advise it. Hopefully, on 15 October 2017, new elections of councils have already joined. The third phase of the reform is expected to start at the end of 2017. During this period, the Government of the Republic should prepare and conduct a merger in those municipalities which do not meet the criteria laid down by law. The process should be completed by the end of 2018. The second question concerns administrative reform: 'What is the administrative reform procedure? Who's getting involved? What is the inclusion process? By what time will the government's vision of the new administration of the country develop? How will discussions between the various political groups and committees of the Riigikogu be conducted? ' In fact, many are involved in the administrative reform. It's hard for me to accept the accusations that started here very lightly. We have plenty of time. As I said, the timetable for the reform has been well-designed enough to allow time for inclusion. This is particularly important during the period of voluntary accession. Nothing's made up in the office or trying to force through it. All the work is still ongoing. Representatives of national self-government associations are involved in both the panel of experts and the government committee. There have been dozens of meetings with local government associations in counties, rural elders, with the involvement of both ministers and officials. Discussion seminars began today, taking place in all counties. At the end of October, we plan to hold a major seminar-conference to introduce the experience of the merger that has already taken place. I have already had meetings with the political groups of the Riigikogu and the following meetings have been agreed. I have communicated and I am ready to communicate with all the forces represented in the Riigikogu. In this sense, we really want a substantive solution. And when you listened, who are also members of the panel of experts, I do have the conviction that this is a very dignified and very experienced group of people, made up of people with political experience, there are no more active politicians there, but, above all, scientists and those who have been conducting mergers in Estonia for ten to fifteen years. So in this sense, involvement is still ongoing. Absolutely every idea, every proposal is welcome and in the Riigikogu we can hopefully then discuss reform plans very thoroughly at the beginning of next year. In the Riigikogu, the commission is, in my knowledge, the Committee on Constitutional Affairs, but it is certainly possible to involve other political groups and committees. That's not a problem. Question 3: Will regionality be required to be taken into account in the assessment of draft laws from now on? Have the impact of excise duties on fuel and VAT increases on regional development been analysed? What are the results? Or is it one of the specific forms of'mandatory reform'? ' It can be said that under the current Code of Good Lawmaking and Standard Techniques, when drafting draft laws, the impact of the implementation of the law, including the impact on regional development, must be explained in the explanatory memorandum. According to the Impact Assessment Review questionnaire, it needs to be assessed whether and how the draft will affect the achievement of the overall objectives of national regional policy, the factors shaping regional development in the different regions, and the local position and participation in both national and local development planning and policy-making processes. As regards excise duties on fuel and VAT on accommodation services, it can be said that these have been political decisions coming from the 100-day programme of the Government of the Republic. The explanatory notes to these drafts have had an impact analysis. In the case of excise duty rates, the main risks to different sectors have certainly been identified. As regards the increase in VAT on tourist accommodation, the experience of the various countries has been analysed in exactly the same way and concluded that the VAT rate is not a determining factor in choosing the tourist destination, there are very many other important factors. We know, for example, that the significant loss of Russian tourists has nothing to do with the VAT rate. But this is a separate issue, I think it is not the main focus of administrative reform issues. The fourth question: 'It is very important to review the responsibilities of the municipalities and the financing issues [---]. When will the evaluation criteria for local governments be developed? Who will develop these criteria? What measures are being taken against those municipalities that do not meet the criteria? ' It can be said that this process should logically look like a panel of experts preparing the main options and alternatives, then discussing them in a government committee, making political decisions, after which the Ministry of Finance will write the draft. There will be regular coordination and procedures in the government and Parliament. We want to agree on these basic criteria, which will become the so-called pillars of administrative reform, already in November this year. There is not too much time to waste and a lot of work and analysis has been done. As explained, in the case of non-compliant municipalities, the Government of the Republic will initiate accession, but hopefully the main wave of accession will take place before that. We will double the merger grants, as has been said, we will advise on these processes, so that money is provided for, and we will increase the social guarantees of the local government leaders. This process should look like this until the end of 2017. In 2018 the government will have to intervene. The experience so far suggests that not everything will happen on its own initiative is likely to escape this virtue. The fifth question probably concerns the reorganisation of the government. It has been asked what effect it is expected to have by restructuring the agencies and moving the departments from one administration to another. Let us say that these reorganisations are linked to political priorities, to the implementation of the same reform. I am not going to talk here at this late hour about restructuring the Ministry of Agriculture into the Ministry of Rural Affairs or moving the Competition Authority into the field of the Ministry of Justice. Let's focus on the key question. From the Ministry of Interior, the Regional Administration Department, Regional Development Department and Planning Department were transferred to the Ministry of Finance area of administration and the county governments co-ordinated by the departments. The cooperation between the Regional Departments of the Ministry of Interior and the Administrative Policy Departments of the Ministry of Finance and the Financial Management Department of Local Authorities is more immediate and operational when areas and competences are institutionally and physically combined. The reform of local government, which involves so many different authorities, is already complex and resource-intensive, and it is therefore good to have basic decisions made and the core core is within the same institution. This will make it easier to prepare for the reform. The provision of expertise in the Department of Local Government Financial Management of the Ministry of Finance under the responsibility of the Minister responsible for administrative reform supports the preparation and implementation of the administrative reform. Similarly, under the Ministry of Finance, there is in fact a supranational administrative organisation, human resource management and development of the public service. So this synergy has arisen. From 1 September we will indeed be together physically and I can assure you that this was the right step. They also asked what it would cost. The reorganisation of the regional area took into account the fact that the area is moving from one area of government to another, together with the budget needed to carry out its activities and the assets in daily use. The material cost of reorganisation involved moving items from one location to another, preparing premises and workplaces, changing IT systems and other administrative costs of €29000 in total. Thank you.

Do you accept such a situation, or does the government intend to intervene in any way and change that picture?
ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U488	Logic is, however, that when we enter the criteria in the law, it is completely real, as the Prime Minister said, that there will be some exceptions that are reasonable and logical, basically the same example of small islands, municipalities fulfilling these criteria, and there will be seven municipalities with 5,000 inhabitants (there may be even more in Harjumaa), there is no reason to say that bullets, we simply do not like subjectively that you have more municipalities here than in neighbouring countries. Honestly, you can't do that. The rules should be in place immediately. The government is still starting to initiate mergers in cases where the criteria are not met, municipalities are still too small or do not meet other substantive criteria. This is where the government intervenes and must unite, not so that we draw some kind of ideal map, and then we leave aside the other criteria. We haven't gotten to drawing a map yet.

ParlaMint-EE_2015-09-21_U513	Thank you. I can assure you that there was no numerical purpose in the negotiations between the government coalition or representatives of the Reform Party. It was the result of the debate, not that we start with a number and then think about the back of the world. Believe me, it was the result of these analyses and debates, the debates, that it would come to a specific phase at one point. And when we had come to this conviction that yes, one criterion can be the number of inhabitants there were several other options: whether it should be the number of working-age residents, etc., then we took time and ordered more expert advice on what the size of the population could be, a specific number. We got several options on the table, as I said in the paper. The first was 3500, which the panel generally considers to be an absolute minimum, so that the municipality can function rationally today. The next step from there is 5,000, which comes out in quite a number of areas. At five thousand, the budget is large enough, the agency is large enough, there are full-time people who can be required to have a corresponding education, etc. This 5000 border also takes some account of the fact that demographic processes have not actually ended in Estonia. Landings are still taking place, the region of Tallinn and Tartu is where the population is growing, and the rest of the world is decreasing. We should look at this reform and the population from the point of view that we will not be in the same place in a year's time, that we would rather look ahead ten to fifteen years. The number was also 11000, where the main logic is that it is the limit of being able to maintain a large modern upper secondary school, which means 256 students for the Ministry of Education. So there were different steps, but in the end it is up to the Riigikogu to decide where it will draw that line if it wants to draw it. In the case of upper secondary schools, a lot has been asked what happens to upper secondary school education, whether it remains the responsibility of the local government or the state. The Ministry of Education and educational strategies are currently moving towards the fact that upper secondary schools tend to go into the hands of the state. And that's why a lot of experts and local government leaders are saying that pulling 11000 to 12,000 is wrong in the sense that if it comes from the ability to maintain upper secondary school while, at the same time, upper secondary schools are going to the state, it's a ridiculous criterion. But basically, it's debatable. I think that if the municipalities really want the upper secondary schools to remain, then you really need to join to reach the 10,000 to 15,000. This has been the case with figures-related discussions. As far as the divisions are concerned, it is possible to do them now. We want to make this regulation more precise, clearer. We want to create an administrative body. Indeed, if the municipality gets larger territorially and there is some very clear area of regional identity or peculiarity that wants to maintain, even for a transitional period, the status of the municipality with its own administration body, then this administrative body could still have a certain right to decide, for example, on the amount of the budget or the regional choices, so that it is not simply a beer room. We want to create this opportunity. I stress that if it takes away any tension or fear, use it. But it remains the choice of the merging municipality itself to decide whether or not to do so. So far, the merged municipalities have not used it, but perhaps, in the context of this reform, we will see more of the birth of these divisions. In the long term, this need may be lost, but in the short term, with four or eight years in mind, you can quite think about what is the function and power of public administration. But yes, indeed, if a division is made, if an administrative body is already set up, then the central unit could also give some decision-making powers. That's the plan. I believe that this seems to be a sympathetic behaviour for the Free Party if inclusion and local democracy do not necessarily have to go any further.
